Open for round 1 critique.\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 1. PURPOSE & SCOPE\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 1.1  S8 owns THREE deliverables:\n\n       1. **C3a UI surfaces** \u2014 Five HTML/JS/CSS pages under\n          `static/c3a/` rendering the C3a state machine for the\n          end-user. Hand-written HTML, vanilla JS, plain CSS;\n          no framework dependency. Mirrors the existing\n          `brief_form.*` (C1 UI) pattern.\n\n       2. **Validation suite (API-level)** \u2014 A new test directory\n          `tests/validation/` containing in-process integration\n          tests that exercise the full C3a state machine through\n          its HTTP surface. Each test runs against a freshly-\n          initialized in-process HTTP server with isolated\n          fixtures (temp DB, mocked email hook, controllable\n          clock). ~50 tests across 7 files.\n\n       3. **Browser-driven e2e tests** \u2014 Playwright tests under\n          `tests/e2e/` that exercise the UI surfaces against a\n          running server. Tests cover the user flows end-to-end:\n          case-selection \u2192 resolution \u2192 cba-verification (with a\n          24h-elapsed simulation), abort flows, fallback resume.\n          ~15 tests.\n\n\u00a7 1.2  Locked-surface discipline.\n\n       (i) S7b SPEC v1.0 LOCKED is invariant. S8 does NOT amend\n           any S7b invariant (P19\u2013P35). If S8 needs behavior\n           outside the S7b surface, that's a spec amendment which\n           should be handled separately \u2014 NOT folded into S8.\n\n       (ii) Already-shipped C3a code (S1\u2013S6, B-027, S7a, S7b) is\n            LOCKED. S8 is additive only:\n            - NEW test files under `tests/validation/`\n            - NEW test files under `tests/e2e/`\n            - NEW static files under `static/c3a/`\n            - NEW server.py routes for serving `static/c3a/*`\n              files (the only production-code change permitted \u2014\n              the routes are mechanically identical to existing\n              `/brief_form.*` routes)\n            - NEW spec section (this document) attached to the\n              spec chain\n            - NEW changelog entries in this document only\n\n       (iii) No production HANDLER code is modified by S8 unless\n             a critique round surfaces a one-line additive\n             omission (analogous to the Session-26 truncation\n             patch). Such patches are ratified by an entry in\n             the spec changelog and tagged PATCH-IN-S8-SCOPE.\n\n\u00a7 1.3  What S8 does NOT cover.\n\n       (a) Performance benchmarks. (Separate session.)\n       (b) Multi-tenant isolation. (No multi-tenancy in v1.)\n       (c) Cross-component (C1\u00d7C3a, C2\u00d7C3a) integration tests.\n           (Owned by separate spec when those integrations are\n           operationalized.)\n       (d) Security audit. (Owned by a future security-pass spec.)\n       (e) Production deployment to a particular target. (Owned\n           by DEPLOY.md, not S8.)\n       (f) Backlog items B-040 through B-053. Each has its own\n           trigger and queue position; S8 does NOT consume\n           backlog work.\n       (g) Mobile-specific layouts. v0.1.1 UI works on mobile\n           via responsive CSS but is not optimized for it.\n       (h) Internationalization. UI is English-only in v1.\n       (i) Accessibility (a11y) audit. UI uses semantic HTML and\n           proper labels but is not WCAG-AA-tested in S8.\n           Capture as backlog if needed.\n       (j) Visual regression / screenshot diffing \u2014 see \u00a7 0.3 (h).\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 2. INVARIANTS (NON-NEGOTIABLE)\n================================================================\n\nS8 introduces six new invariants (P36\u2013P41) that extend the S7b\nchain. None modify S7b's P19\u2013P35.\n\n\u00a7 2.1  P36 \u2014 Validation tests are deterministic.\n\n       Every test in `tests/validation/` and `tests/e2e/` produces\n       the same result on every run, in every order, on every\n       machine running Python 3.10+ with the project's pinned\n       deps.\n\n       Mechanism:\n       - Time is controlled via a `FrozenClock` fixture that\n         makes time.time() and int(time.time()) return\n         deterministic values.\n       - Random-token generators are seeded for the test session.\n       - SQLite uses `:memory:` or `tempfile` (no shared state\n         between tests).\n       - HTTP responses don't depend on real network (Resend\n         hook is mocked via `unittest.mock.patch` per S7b \u00a7 8.6).\n       - E2E tests use deterministic test seeds for Playwright.\n\n       Verification: re-run the suite 10 times in random order;\n       ZERO flakes acceptable.\n\n\u00a7 2.2  P37 \u2014 Validation tests run in under 90 seconds total.\n\n       The full `tests/validation/` + `tests/e2e/` suite executes\n       in \u2264 90 sec on a developer laptop. Rationale: integration\n       tests that take longer get skipped or run-eventually,\n       defeating their purpose.\n\n       Budget split:\n       - tests/validation/ (~50 tests): \u2264 60 sec\n       - tests/e2e/ (~15 tests, includes browser warmup): \u2264 30 sec\n\n       If P37 is at risk, address by:\n       - Sharing a single in-process HTTP server across tests\n         in the same module (setUpClass / setUpModule / pytest\n         fixture session scope).\n       - Using `:memory:` SQLite where the test doesn't need\n         persistence across in-process actors.\n       - Reusing the playwright browser context across e2e tests\n         in the same module.\n       - NOT by parallelizing test execution. Determinism (P36)\n         > speed.\n\n\u00a7 2.3  P38 \u2014 Validation tests are HERMETIC.\n\n       Test execution does not require:\n       - Network access\n       - A running Resend account\n       - A specific machine clock value\n       - Environment variables beyond what the test sets itself\n         (in particular: NO RESEND_API_KEY required; NO\n         BUILDEMUP_DATABASE_PATH required)\n       - File-system locations outside tempfile.gettempdir()\n\n       Playwright e2e tests require the chromium binary\n       (one-time `playwright install chromium` per dev machine\n       and per CI runner) but the test execution itself does not\n       need network beyond localhost.\n\n       Hermetic = reproducible = can run anywhere.\n\n\u00a7 2.4  P39 \u2014 Validation tests document the C3a contract.\n\n       Each test class has a docstring naming the invariant or\n       behavior under test in spec terms. A reader who finds a\n       test failure can identify which P-invariant or which \u00a7\n       section is being exercised without reading the test body.\n\n       Operational benefit: when production behavior drifts from\n       spec, the failing test names which contract is being\n       violated.\n\n\u00a7 2.5  P40 \u2014 UI tests exercise UI through a real browser engine.\n\n       E2E tests use Playwright + chromium against a live\n       in-process server. They do NOT use jsdom, JSDOMs, or any\n       DOM-mock library. The browser engine catches real-world\n       issues (layout, focus management, form submission encoding,\n       redirect handling) that mocks cannot.\n\n       Cost: one-time `playwright install chromium` per\n       dev/CI machine. Worth it for the realism guarantee.\n\n\u00a7 2.6  P41 \u2014 UI tests use semantic selectors.\n\n       Selectors prefer (in order):\n       (a) ARIA role + accessible name: `page.get_by_role(\"button\",\n           name=\"Confirm\")`\n       (b) Form label: `page.get_by_label(\"Email\")`\n       (c) Visible text: `page.get_by_text(\"Resolve case\")`\n       (d) test-id attributes (data-testid=\"...\") for genuinely\n           unique non-textual elements.\n\n       Selectors do NOT use:\n       - Brittle CSS path selectors (`.container > .card:nth-child(2)`)\n       - Auto-generated class names\n       - XPath in any form\n\n       Tests survive cosmetic CSS changes by depending on\n       semantics, not layout.\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 3. ARCHITECTURE\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 3.1  File layout (S8 adds):\n\n       ```\n       buildemup/\n         static/\n           c3a/                            [NEW S8]\n             case.html                     -- question + options view\n             case.js\n             case.css\n             checklist.html                -- cba-checklist + resume\n             checklist.js\n             checklist.css\n             done.html                     -- terminal-success view\n             done.js                       (minimal; mostly static)\n             aborted.html                  -- abort confirmation\n             aborted.js                    (minimal; mostly static)\n             _shared.css                   -- shared design tokens\n             _shared.js                    -- trace_id + fetch helpers\n         tests/\n           validation/                     [NEW S8]\n             __init__.py\n             conftest.py                   -- shared pytest fixtures\n             test_c3a_state_machine.py     -- happy-path traversal\n             test_c3a_idempotency.py       -- P12 + P21 + P28\n             test_c3a_termination.py       -- 5 terminal states\n             test_c3a_24h_fallback.py      -- scheduler chain\n             test_c3a_admin_recovery.py    -- admin reset paths\n             test_c3a_observability.py     -- /health + metrics\n             test_c3a_failure_modes.py     -- 503 / 4xx / 5xx\n           e2e/                            [NEW S8]\n             __init__.py\n             conftest.py                   -- playwright fixtures\n             playwright.config.json\n             test_c3a_browser_happy_flow.py\n             test_c3a_browser_cba_flow.py\n             test_c3a_browser_abort.py\n             test_c3a_browser_resume.py\n             test_c3a_browser_failure.py\n       ```\n\n       Plus modifications to existing files:\n\n       ```\n         api/\n           server.py                       -- add 5 GET routes for\n                                              static/c3a/* serving\n                                              (mechanical extension\n                                              of existing pattern)\n       ```\n\n\u00a7 3.2  Fixture architecture (validation suite).\n\n       Tests use a layered fixture model:\n\n       (a) `temp_db_path` (function-scoped) \u2014 produces a unique\n           SQLite path under tempfile.gettempdir(). Cleanup on\n           teardown.\n\n       (b) `fresh_storage(temp_db_path)` \u2014 initializes\n           GateStateStorage and SchedulerStateStorage against\n           that path, with `_ensure_schema` applied. Returns a\n           tuple. Resets the singleton injection points in the\n           api modules so tests don't leak storage between runs.\n\n       (c) `frozen_clock(t0=...)` \u2014 patches time.time and\n           datetime.utcnow to return controllable values. Tests\n           manipulate the clock with `frozen_clock.advance(secs)`\n           rather than time.sleep. Required for the 24h fallback\n           test.\n\n       (d) `live_server(fresh_storage, frozen_clock)` \u2014 spins up\n           an in-process HTTPServer thread on a free port. Yields\n           a base URL. Teardown does srv.shutdown() + thread.join.\n           Module-scoped where individual tests don't need\n           isolation, function-scoped where they do.\n\n       (e) `mocked_resend(live_server)` \u2014 patches urllib.request\n           so the email hook records calls without sending.\n\n       (f) `seeded_brief(fresh_storage, brief_token=..., ...)` \u2014\n           inserts a `gate_states` row representing a previously-\n           captured brief, so c3a tests start from a valid\n           C1-output state without coupling to C1.\n\n       Fixtures live in `tests/validation/conftest.py`. pytest's\n       fixture model is the host (we already use pytest project-\n       wide).\n\n\u00a7 3.3  Fixture architecture (e2e suite).\n\n       E2E tests reuse the validation fixtures plus playwright-\n       specific ones:\n\n       (g) `browser` (session-scoped) \u2014 playwright Browser\n           instance. One per test session. Headless by default;\n           `PLAYWRIGHT_HEADLESS=false` for local debugging.\n\n       (h) `context(browser)` (function-scoped) \u2014 fresh\n           BrowserContext per test (cookies, storage isolated\n           per test).\n\n       (i) `page(context)` (function-scoped) \u2014 Playwright Page.\n\n       (j) `c3a_test_environment` \u2014 wraps fresh_storage +\n           live_server + mocked_resend + frozen_clock + browser\n           into one fixture for e2e tests so each test gets the\n           full stack.\n\n       Fixtures live in `tests/e2e/conftest.py`.\n\n\u00a7 3.4  Test seams (what's mocked vs real):\n\n       REAL:\n       - SchedulerStateStorage and GateStateStorage (against temp\n         SQLite) \u2014 these ARE under test.\n       - All five c3a_endpoint handlers \u2014 under test.\n       - admin_endpoint, health_endpoint, server.py \u2014 under test.\n       - JSON parsing, HTTP framing, content-length, CORS headers.\n       - Static file serving (under test for the new c3a routes).\n       - Playwright's chromium browser (real DOM, real CSS, real JS).\n\n       MOCKED:\n       - Resend email hook (urllib.request.urlopen). Test\n         captures the constructed Request to assert on shape;\n         the hook's action is recorded but no network call.\n       - `time.time()` and `datetime.utcnow()` in tests that\n         exercise time-window logic.\n\n       NEITHER REAL NOR MOCKED (i.e., not exercised at all):\n       - C1 (brief capture). Tests seed brief_token rows into\n         gate_states directly via fixture.\n       - C2 (feasibility). Out of S8 scope.\n       - The Resend service. Hook is mocked; nothing reaches\n         the real Resend.\n\n\u00a7 3.5  Test data strategy.\n\n       (a) Brief tokens are deterministic UUIDs based on test\n           name (e.g., `brief-test_happy_path-001`) so failures\n           are debuggable.\n       (b) Session tokens come from the real handler responses\n           (not synthesized) so the format-version chain stays\n           realistic.\n       (c) Fixture brief_token data uses minimal valid JSON\n           shapes \u2014 just enough to satisfy gate_state validators.\n       (d) No real PII in test data. Email addresses are of the\n           form `test+<test_name>@example.test` (a TLD reserved\n           by RFC 2606 for testing).\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 4. TEST PLAN \u2014 VALIDATION SUITE\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 4.1  Test count target.\n\n       v0.1.1 DRAFT estimate:\n       - tests/validation/ : ~50 tests across 7 files\n       - tests/e2e/        : ~15 tests across 5 files\n       - Total             : ~65 new tests\n\n       Final count emerges from critique rounds.\n\n\u00a7 4.2  test_c3a_state_machine.py (~10 tests).\n\n       Walks the C3a state machine end-to-end via HTTP:\n       - check \u2192 CASE_PENDING \u2192 resolve \u2192 terminal\n       - check \u2192 CASE_PENDING \u2192 resolve \u2192 CBA_VERIFICATION_PAUSED\n         \u2192 cba-verified \u2192 terminal\n       - check \u2192 CASE_PENDING \u2192 resolve \u2192 CBA_VERIFICATION_PAUSED\n         \u2192 (24h elapsed via frozen_clock) \u2192 admin tick \u2192\n         cba-fallback-continue invoked \u2192 terminal\n       - check \u2192 CASE_PENDING \u2192 abort \u2192 USER_ABORTED\n       - Various option-id permutations\n       - State transitions verified via direct gate_states reads\n\n\u00a7 4.3  test_c3a_idempotency.py (~7 tests).\n\n       P12, P21, P28 verified end-to-end:\n       - Same brief_token + request_id on /check returns same\n         session_token (P12 uniqueness).\n       - Same session_token + request_id on /resolve returns\n         cached response (P21 idempotency).\n       - Two parallel admin/scheduler/tick calls claim distinct\n         rows from scheduler_state (P28 atomic dequeue).\n       - Same request_id on cba-fallback-continue returns cached\n         response.\n       - Idempotency across server restart (DB persistence).\n\n\u00a7 4.4  test_c3a_termination.py (~7 tests).\n\n       Each of the 5 terminal states reachable + properly\n       finalized:\n       - TERMINAL_OK\n       - USER_ABORTED\n       - CBA_VERIFIED\n       - CBA_FALLBACK_FIRED\n       - SCHEMA_DEPRECATED (shape only \u2014 synthetic)\n       - is_terminal=1 set in DB\n       - Subsequent operations on terminal sessions return\n         410 Gone with code `terminal`\n\n\u00a7 4.5  test_c3a_24h_fallback.py (~8 tests).\n\n       The full P21 + P33 24h-fallback chain:\n       - check \u2192 resolve \u2192 CBA_VERIFICATION_PAUSED enqueues a\n         scheduler_state row with fire_at = now + 86400.\n       - frozen_clock.advance(86400 + 60).\n       - admin/scheduler/tick claims it, posts to\n         /cba-fallback-continue, commits fired.\n       - Failed POST \u2192 record_error \u2192 re-claim after cooldown.\n       - 3 failed POSTs \u2192 row stuck (P33), surfaced via /health.\n       - admin/scheduler/reset clears stuck row.\n       - Email hook called with right payload + trace_id.\n\n\u00a7 4.6  test_c3a_admin_recovery.py (~5 tests).\n\n       P35 admin reset paths exhaustively:\n       - RESET outcome on stuck row.\n       - ALREADY_FIRED on committed row.\n       - NOT_FOUND on unknown token.\n       - Auth failure: missing token \u2192 401.\n       - Auth failure: wrong token \u2192 401.\n\n\u00a7 4.7  test_c3a_observability.py (~6 tests).\n\n       /health + metrics surface:\n       - GET /health returns 200 + diagnostics in healthy state.\n       - GET /health returns 503 + code on failed write check.\n       - scheduler_due_count + scheduler_stuck_count reflect\n         real queue state.\n       - Each handler emits exactly one structured-log metric\n         line per call.\n       - Metric line schema is exactly the closed P30 vocabulary.\n       - Trace_id propagated end-to-end through metric lines.\n\n\u00a7 4.8  test_c3a_failure_modes.py (~7 tests).\n\n       Failure paths produce correct HTTP behavior:\n       - SQLite \"database is locked\" \u2192 503 + storage_busy +\n         Retry-After: 5.\n       - Oversized request body \u2192 400 + request_too_large.\n       - Malformed JSON \u2192 400 + bad_json.\n       - Missing required field \u2192 400 + validation.\n       - Unknown session_token \u2192 404 + unknown_token.\n       - Action on terminal session \u2192 410 + terminal.\n       - X-Trace-Id malformed \u2192 fresh trace_id + WARNING log.\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 5. UI SURFACES\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 5.1  Pages overview.\n\n       Five pages under `static/c3a/`. Each is a discrete URL\n       (no client-side routing per Q9); browser back/forward,\n       bookmarks, deep links all work natively.\n\n       (i)   /c3a/case.html?brief=<token>\n             Initial entry point. Calls /api/extreme-case/check\n             with the brief token. Renders the surfaced extreme\n             case + options as radio choices. User selects an\n             option + clicks \"Resolve\". POSTs to\n             /api/extreme-case/resolve. Response either redirects\n             to /c3a/done (terminal) or /c3a/checklist (paused).\n\n       (ii)  /c3a/checklist.html?session=<token>\n             Renders the CBA verification checklist for a paused\n             case. Two paths: user clicks \"I have completed CBA\"\n             \u2192 POSTs to /api/extreme-case/cba-verified, redirects\n             to /c3a/done; OR user is here from an email link\n             24h+ later \u2014 same UX.\n\n       (iii) /c3a/done.html?session=<token>\n             Terminal success view. Reads is_terminal + \n             terminal_state from a small status fetch (or pulls\n             from URL state). Shows confirmation + the terminal\n             state label.\n\n       (iv)  /c3a/aborted.html?session=<token>\n             Abort confirmation. Reached when user clicks \"Abort\n             this case\" on the case page.\n\n       (v)   /c3a/_shared.css and /c3a/_shared.js (technically\n             not pages, but shared assets all 4 pages reference).\n\n\u00a7 5.2  Page-specific behavior \u2014 case.html.\n\n       On load:\n       - Read `brief` query param.\n       - POST /api/extreme-case/check {brief_token: <token>,\n         request_id: <new-uuid>}.\n       - On 200: render `extreme_case.case_text` as the prompt;\n         render `extreme_case.options` as a radio list with\n         option_id as values. Stash session_token in a hidden\n         field.\n       - On 4xx: show error UX with trace_id.\n       - On 503: show \"checking...\" with spinner; auto-retry\n         honoring Retry-After header.\n\n       On submit:\n       - \"Resolve\" button \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/resolve\n         {session_token, option_id, request_id}.\n       - On 200 with is_terminal=true: window.location =\n         \"/c3a/done.html?session=<token>\".\n       - On 200 with is_terminal=false (paused state): redirect\n         to \"/c3a/checklist.html?session=<token>\".\n       - \"Abort\" button \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/abort\n         {session_token, request_id}; redirect to\n         /c3a/aborted.html.\n\n\u00a7 5.3  Page-specific behavior \u2014 checklist.html.\n\n       On load:\n       - Read `session` query param.\n       - Render the static CBA checklist content (questions\n         like \"have you completed structural verification?\",\n         \"load test signed off?\", etc.) \u2014 content is hardcoded\n         in the page; no API call needed for the checklist\n         items themselves.\n       - Show a single \"I confirm CBA is complete\" button.\n\n       On submit:\n       - POST /api/extreme-case/cba-verified {session_token,\n         request_id, verified: true}.\n       - On 200: redirect to /c3a/done.\n       - On 410 (already terminal): show \"this case is already\n         closed\" + redirect to /c3a/done after delay.\n       - On 4xx: show error UX with trace_id.\n\n\u00a7 5.4  Page-specific behavior \u2014 done.html and aborted.html.\n\n       Both are minimal. On load, render a static success/abort\n       confirmation. Optional: small status fetch via a future\n       /api/extreme-case/status endpoint. v0.1.1 doesn't add\n       such an endpoint (Pattern E counter); pages render\n       confirmation from URL state alone.\n\n       Both pages link back to \"Start a new brief\" (= C1 form).\n\n\u00a7 5.5  Design language baseline (Q8 author position).\n\n       Match the existing brief_form.css aesthetic:\n       - System font stack (no web fonts loaded).\n       - Single-column layout, max-width ~640px centered.\n       - Form-focused: large clickable areas, clear labels,\n         visible focus states.\n       - Color: muted neutrals + one accent color for primary\n         actions. Status colors (red/orange/green) for\n         error/warning/success.\n       - No CSS framework. Hand-written ~200 LOC across all\n         pages, per S8 budget.\n       - Responsive: works on mobile via simple media queries\n         but is not mobile-optimized per \u00a7 1.3 (g).\n       - Accessibility: semantic HTML, proper labels, ARIA\n         roles where needed. No automated a11y audit per\n         \u00a7 1.3 (i).\n\n\u00a7 5.6  Shared assets \u2014 _shared.js.\n\n       Provides:\n       - `traceId()` \u2014 returns a stable 16-hex trace_id for\n         the current page-load (stored in sessionStorage so\n         it persists across the SPA-but-not-SPA flow).\n       - `c3aFetch(url, opts)` \u2014 wraps fetch() to:\n         - inject X-Trace-Id header automatically.\n         - inject Content-Type: application/json on POSTs.\n         - parse JSON response.\n         - on 503 + Retry-After: auto-retry once after\n           waiting Retry-After seconds.\n         - throw a typed error on non-2xx with body parsed.\n       - `displayError(err, traceId)` \u2014 renders an error UI\n         inside a target element. Used by all pages.\n\n\u00a7 5.7  Shared assets \u2014 _shared.css.\n\n       Provides design tokens (colors, spacing, font sizes)\n       plus baseline element styles (button, input, .card,\n       .error). All page CSS files import _shared.css.\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 6. CONTRACT DOCUMENT \u2014 UI \u2190\u2192 API\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 6.1  Endpoints C3a UI consumes.\n\n       POST /api/extreme-case/check\n       POST /api/extreme-case/resolve\n       POST /api/extreme-case/abort\n       POST /api/extreme-case/cba-verified\n\n       Plus, NOT consumed by UI but mentioned for completeness:\n       POST /api/extreme-case/cba-fallback-continue\n            (USED BY THE SCHEDULER)\n\n\u00a7 6.2  Request/response shapes.\n\n       Each endpoint's shape is fully specified in the\n       c3a_endpoint module docstrings + S7a SPEC \u00a7 5. The UI\n       contract is: those shapes are the source of truth and\n       won't change without a new C3a sub-session spec.\n\n       Headers the UI MUST send:\n       - Content-Type: application/json on POSTs.\n       - X-Trace-Id: <16-hex> (always, via _shared.js\n         c3aFetch helper).\n\n       Headers the UI MUST handle:\n       - Retry-After (on 503 storage_busy responses).\n       - Standard CORS headers (Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *\n         is set by server.py).\n\n\u00a7 6.3  Status code semantics for UI consumers:\n\n       200 \u2014 success. Body always JSON object with `ok: true`.\n       400 \u2014 validation. Body has `errors: [{code, message}]`.\n       401 \u2014 auth. Admin endpoints only; UI shouldn't see this.\n       404 \u2014 unknown_token. Show \"session not found\" UX.\n       409 \u2014 already_fired (admin reset only).\n       410 \u2014 terminal. Show \"this case is closed\" UX.\n       503 \u2014 storage_busy. Honor Retry-After; show transient\n             error UX via _shared.js c3aFetch helper.\n\n\u00a7 6.4  Trace_id propagation expectation.\n\n       UI generates a 16-hex trace_id at the start of a user\n       session (via _shared.js traceId()), sends it on every\n       C3a call, and surfaces it in any error UI (\"if you\n       contact support, share this code: <trace_id>\").\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 7. REQUEST-RESPONSE FLOWS (UI flows)\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 7.1  Happy path:\n\n       1. User arrives at /c3a/case.html?brief=<token>.\n       2. case.js \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/check \u2192 200 with\n          surfaced case + options.\n       3. User selects option, clicks Resolve.\n       4. case.js \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/resolve \u2192 200 with\n          is_terminal=true.\n       5. window.location \u2192 /c3a/done.html?session=<token>.\n\n\u00a7 7.2  Pause-and-continue path (24h):\n\n       1. User arrives at /c3a/case.html, selects option that\n          requires CBA verification.\n       2. case.js \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/resolve \u2192 200 with\n          is_terminal=false, paused_state=CBA_VERIFICATION_PAUSED.\n       3. window.location \u2192 /c3a/checklist.html?session=<token>.\n       4. User reads checklist. Closes browser without confirming.\n       5. (User receives email N hours later with link back to\n          /c3a/checklist.html?session=<token>.)\n       6. User clicks email link, returns to checklist page.\n       7. User clicks \"I confirm\".\n       8. checklist.js \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/cba-verified \u2192\n          200 with is_terminal=true.\n       9. window.location \u2192 /c3a/done.html?session=<token>.\n\n\u00a7 7.3  Abort path:\n\n       1. User arrives at /c3a/case.html, decides to abort.\n       2. User clicks Abort.\n       3. case.js \u2192 POST /api/extreme-case/abort \u2192 200 with\n          is_terminal=true, terminal_state=USER_ABORTED.\n       4. window.location \u2192 /c3a/aborted.html?session=<token>.\n\n\u00a7 7.4  Failure handling:\n\n       On 503 + Retry-After:\n         _shared.js c3aFetch waits Retry-After seconds and\n         retries the SAME request (same request_id). P21\n         idempotency guarantees no duplicate side-effects.\n         If the second attempt also fails, displayError() is\n         called.\n\n       On 4xx other than 503:\n         displayError() shows the error message from\n         body.errors[0].message. Includes body.trace_id.\n         Does NOT auto-retry (4xx is client-side; auto-retry\n         would just amplify the error).\n\n       On 5xx other than 503:\n         displayError() shows a generic \"system error\" with\n         trace_id. Logs to console for debug.\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 8. EXECUTION & TIMING\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 8.1  Test wall-clock budget: 90 seconds total (P37).\n\n       Distributed roughly:\n       - state_machine ~10s\n       - idempotency ~5s\n       - termination ~5s\n       - 24h_fallback ~15s\n       - admin_recovery ~5s\n       - observability ~10s\n       - failure_modes ~5s\n       - validation fixture init/teardown overhead ~5s\n       - e2e suite (5 files, ~15 tests, browser warmup) ~30s\n\n\u00a7 8.2  CI integration.\n\n       Validation tests run in the same pytest invocation as\n       the existing project-wide tests. NO separate test phase.\n       This is enforced by the standard pytest discovery rules.\n\n       E2E tests can be skipped if `playwright` is not\n       installed in the environment (`pytest.importorskip`).\n       In CI, `playwright install chromium` is a setup step.\n\n       After S8 ships:\n       ```\n       python -m pytest buildemup/tests -q\n       # \u2192 expected: 1182 + ~65 = ~1247 passed\n       ```\n\n\u00a7 8.3  Smoke test interaction.\n\n       `smoke_c3a_deploy.py` (shipped Session 26) is unchanged.\n       It's the post-deploy verification surface; this validation\n       suite is the in-process pre-deploy verification surface.\n       Both coexist without overlap.\n\n\u00a7 8.4  Local dev workflow.\n\n       Developer iterating on UI:\n       1. Start the server: `python -m buildemup.api.server`\n       2. Navigate to http://localhost:8000/c3a/case.html?brief=...\n       3. Make changes to static/c3a/*; reload browser.\n\n       Developer iterating on validation tests:\n       1. `python -m pytest tests/validation/ -v -k <test_name>`\n\n       Developer iterating on e2e tests:\n       1. `PLAYWRIGHT_HEADLESS=false python -m pytest tests/e2e/\n          -v -k <test_name>` (browser opens for visual debug)\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 9. SCHEMA & ROLLOUT\n================================================================\n\n\u00a7 9.1  No schema changes.\n\n       S8 is purely additive in tests + static files + (small,\n       mechanical) server.py route additions. No changes to:\n       - gate_states schema\n       - scheduler_state schema\n       - any production handler module\n       - any environment variable\n       - any HTTP API endpoint shape (only ADDS static-file\n         GET routes)\n\n\u00a7 9.2  Rollback strategy.\n\n       S8 ships when validation suite + e2e suite go green and\n       Ramalingam confirms. If a critical issue surfaces post-\n       S8-ship:\n\n       (a) UI bug: revert the static/c3a/ commit. API is\n           untouched. Server still serves the existing\n           brief_form pages.\n       (b) Test bug: revert the tests/validation/ or tests/e2e/\n           commit. Production code is untouched.\n       (c) Server route bug: revert the server.py addition.\n           Static C3a pages return 404 but no other routes\n           affected.\n\n       In all cases, rollback is partial and preserves\n       already-working surfaces.\n\n\u00a7 9.3  Deploy preparation.\n\n       Before S8 ships to production:\n       - Add `playwright` to dev/CI requirements.txt (NOT\n         production requirements; UI runs in user browsers).\n       - Add `playwright install chromium` to CI setup script.\n       - Update DEPLOY.md to mention the new C3a UI URLs (e.g.,\n         \"/c3a/case.html?brief=<token> is the entry point after\n         C1 brief capture completes\").\n\n\u00a7 9.4  Backward compatibility.\n\n       Trivially preserved (additive only).\n\n================================================================\n\u00a7 10.     "text": "**v0.1.1 issued \u2014 1072 lines.** Reading (a) inlined as first-class content, no more `[Q1-(a) ADDS:]` notes.\n\nKey changes from v0.1: three deliverables (was one), 6 active invariants P36\u2013P41 (was 4), section structure re-numbered to give UI its own first-class sections (\u00a7 5 UI surfaces, \u00a7 6 UI\u2194API contract, \u00a7 7 flows). Test count target: ~50 API + ~15 e2e = ~65 total. New questions Q8/Q9/Q10 surfaced for UI-specific concerns (styling baseline, SPA vs page-nav, checklist URL structure).\n\nNow **running critique round 1** \u2014 playing the adversarial critic against my own draft. Findings batched by severity and grouped where related so adjudication is efficient.\n\n---\n\n# S8 SPEC v0.1.1 \u2014 Critique Round 1\n\n## CRITICAL findings (block LOCK)\n\n### Finding 1 \u2014 \u00a7 5.6 hidden architectural decision: trace_id stored in sessionStorage\n\n> \"`traceId()` \u2014 returns a stable 16-hex trace_id for the current page-load (stored in sessionStorage so it persists across the SPA-but-not-SPA flow).\"\n\nThe 24h-fallback flow says the user closes the browser and comes back via email link. **sessionStorage is cleared on browser close.** So the trace_id the user has on the cba-checklist resume page will NOT be the same trace_id that started the case. This breaks the support narrative (\"share this code with support to track your whole interaction\") for exactly the flow it's most needed in.\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) Use localStorage scoped per session_token. Requires cleanup logic.\n- (b) Embed the trace_id in the email link URL itself. Server includes it. Page reads from query param.\n- (c) Accept that trace_id is per-page-load, not per-case. Document this. Support narrative becomes \"share this code AND your email\" or similar.\n\n**My recommendation: (b).** Cleanest. Server already has the original trace_id on the scheduler_state row. The email-link constructor includes it as `?session=<token>&trace=<trace_id>`. checklist.js reads it from URL. No storage needed.\n\n**Severity: CRITICAL** because it breaks a stated S8 invariant (P39 \u2014 tests document the contract; here the contract itself is broken).\n\n### Finding 2 \u2014 \u00a7 7.2 step 5 hand-waves email-link content\n\n> \"(User receives email N hours later with link back to /c3a/checklist.html?session=<token>.)\"\n\nWho constructs that URL? The Resend email body is built by `c3a_email_hook.send_cba_checklist`. That code currently has no awareness of UI URLs at all \u2014 it was built S7b-Phase-3 with placeholder text. **The URL the user clicks must be:**\n- Constructed at email-send time\n- Include the right base URL (production vs dev)\n- Include session_token\n- Include trace_id (per Finding 1)\n- Be backwards-compatible with whatever the email body currently contains\n\nThis is a real production-code change to `c3a_email_hook.py`. **It's not in S8 scope** as currently written (\"S8 is additive only\" per \u00a7 1.2 (ii)) \u2014 but the UI doesn't work without it.\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) Add to S8 scope: small modification to `c3a_email_hook.send_cba_checklist` to construct the URL.\n- (b) Treat as a separate spec-amendment that S8 depends on. Explicitly LOCK S8 with a \"blocked on email-link patch\" status.\n- (c) Hardcode the URL in the email hook now and document it as a Session-26 critique-round patch (analogous to truncation patch).\n\n**My recommendation: (a).** Add an explicit \u00a7 1.2 carve-out: \"S8 may modify c3a_email_hook.py to construct the cba-checklist URL.\" It's a 5-line patch, contained, mechanical.\n\n**Severity: CRITICAL** because the entire 24h fallback UX flow depends on it.\n\n### Finding 3 \u2014 \u00a7 5.2 case.html does POST on page load\n\n> \"On load: POST /api/extreme-case/check {brief_token: <token>, request_id: <new-uuid>}.\"\n\n**This violates HTTP semantics for navigation.** A user landing on case.html via a normal GET navigation triggers a POST as a side effect. Implications:\n- Browser back/forward navigation re-triggers the POST \u2192 idempotency saves us, but it's still wrong-shaped.\n- Refresh re-triggers the POST \u2192 same.\n- Server logs show every page load as a POST \u2192 metrics distorted.\n- A bot/crawler that lands on the URL triggers a POST \u2192 creates a session_token in the DB.\n\nThe right shape: case.html is a `GET /api/extreme-case/check?brief=<token>&request_id=<...>` page-load, not a POST.\n\n**But:** /api/extreme-case/check is a POST in the S7b LOCKED API. Changing it is a spec amendment outside S8 scope.\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) Live with the POST-on-load. Mitigate via P21 idempotency (which already handles it). Document as a known wart.\n- (b) Add a thin GET wrapper endpoint `/api/extreme-case/check-get?brief=...&request_id=...` that internally calls the POST handler. UI uses GET. Adds one route; S7b API surface unchanged.\n- (c) Move the case-creation step server-side: brief-form.js POSTs to /api/extreme-case/check directly when the brief is captured (in C1's flow), and the response includes the session_token. Then case.html is invoked with `?session=<token>` already in hand and just GETs the case data. Cleanest but couples C1 to C3a UI semantics.\n\n**My recommendation: (a) + a documented note.** Don't expand S7b's API surface mid-S8. The POST-on-load is ugly but P21 + idempotency make it safe. Document it as a known limitation; revisit if it bites.\n\n**Severity: CRITICAL** because it's a foundational architectural choice that shapes 5 pages of UI logic.\n\n## IMPORTANT findings (need resolution before LOCK)\n\n### Finding 4 \u2014 \u00a7 5.4 done.html and aborted.html have no API contract\n\nThe pages \"render confirmation from URL state alone.\" But:\n- A user who refreshes done.html with a stale session_token sees a success page even if the session was reset/expired.\n- A user who manually navigates to /c3a/done.html?session=fake sees a success page.\n- No way to distinguish a real terminal session from a fake one in the UI.\n\nThis isn't a security issue (no actions taken from these pages) but it's a UX correctness issue \u2014 and an **adversarial-tester finding** (e.g., a customer support call where a user \"confirms\" they completed CBA but actually didn't \u2014 UI happily said \"done\").\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) Live with it. Stateless terminal pages are a common pattern.\n- (b) Add a small status fetch on these pages: GET /api/extreme-case/status?session=<token>. New endpoint outside S7b LOCKED. Spec-amendment territory.\n- (c) The redirect to done.html includes a signed token (e.g., HMAC of session_token + terminal_state) that the page verifies. No new endpoint, no API change. Adds shared signing key between server and... wait, the page can't verify HMAC without the key. Doesn't work for static pages.\n- (d) The redirect URL includes a one-time view token issued by the server in the resolve response. Page validates by calling a new GET endpoint. Same as (b).\n\n**My recommendation: (a).** Accept the wart. Add a \u00a7 1.3 note documenting it. The new-endpoint paths all violate \u00a7 1.2 (locked surface).\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT** but not blocking. UX wart, not a correctness break.\n\n### Finding 5 \u2014 \u00a7 8.1 budget arithmetic doesn't sum to 90s\n\nListed sub-budgets: 10 + 5 + 5 + 15 + 5 + 10 + 5 + 5 + 30 = **90s exactly**. Good news: it sums. But there's no slack for variance. A slow CI runner blowing the budget by 10% breaks P37.\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) Increase budget to 120s. Round number, allows ~33% slack.\n- (b) Keep 90s but add a CI-only override (`SLOW_RUNNER=1 \u2192 budget=180s`).\n- (c) Drop the budget invariant entirely. Just monitor.\n\n**My recommendation: (a) \u2014 120s budget.** Round number, accommodates real-world CI variance, still well under \"tests no one runs because they're slow\" threshold.\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT** because P37 is an active invariant and a CI flake from budget overrun would be misleading.\n\n### Finding 6 \u2014 \u00a7 3.2 fixture (f) `seeded_brief` couples S8 to gate_states schema\n\n> \"inserts a `gate_states` row representing a previously-captured brief, so c3a tests start from a valid C1-output state without coupling to C1.\"\n\nThis intentionally avoids C1 coupling but creates **schema coupling** instead. Every time gate_states schema changes (e.g., a new column added in some future C3 sub-session), every S8 fixture call needs updating. This is the same drift problem the critique was trying to avoid by not depending on C1.\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) Live with it. Schema changes are rare; the cost is bounded.\n- (b) Use the public GateStateStorage API (the `save()` method) instead of raw SQL inserts. Decouples from schema, adds dependency on the public API instead.\n- (c) Build a `BriefStateBuilder` test helper that wraps either approach.\n\n**My recommendation: (b).** The S8 spec should specify that `seeded_brief` uses `GateStateStorage.save()` not raw inserts. Slightly more code in the fixture, dramatically less drift.\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT** because schema drift is a common test-suite rot pattern.\n\n### Finding 7 \u2014 \u00a7 5.6 c3aFetch retries 503 ONCE \u2014 what about persistent 503?\n\n> \"on 503 + Retry-After: auto-retry once after waiting Retry-After seconds.\"\n\nA persistent SQLite lock issue results in 503 + retry + 503 + give-up. The user sees an error message after waiting Retry-After seconds (= 5s by default per S7b spec \u00a7 6.2). 5s is a long time to make a user wait for a confusing error. But retrying more times makes it worse.\n\n**Options:**\n- (a) One retry, give up, show error UI. (Current spec.)\n- (b) Two retries with exponential backoff (5s, 10s). User waits 15s but more likely to succeed.\n- (c) Show \"checking...\" UI immediately (don't wait for Retry-After to elapse with a blank screen) so the user knows the system is working.\n\n**My recommendation: (a) + (c) combined.** Keep one retry, but show the \"checking...\" state immediately so user has visual feedback during the wait.\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT** for UX quality.\n\n## NICE-TO-HAVE findings (defensible to ship without)\n\n### Finding 8 \u2014 \u00a7 5.1 routes use .html extension\n\n`/c3a/case.html` is functional but stylistically dated. Modern static-served pages typically use `/c3a/case` (clean URL). However, this requires the server to handle the URL \u2192 file mapping (`.html` is the natural Python `http.server` default). v0.1.1 is consistent with the existing `brief_form.html` pattern, so this is just stylistic.\n\n**My recommendation: PUSHBACK.** Consistency with brief_form.html wins. .html extension stays.\n\n### Finding 9 \u2014 \u00a7 5.5 \"Hand-written ~200 LOC across all pages\"\n\nThat's a wildly optimistic estimate for 5 pages with shared design tokens, error handling, form validation, retry logic, and state-specific behavior. Realistic estimate is probably 600-1000 LOC across HTML+JS+CSS for all 5 pages.\n\n**My recommendation: Update estimate to \"~800 LOC\" in v0.2.** Not a spec change, just an honest number.\n\n### Finding 10 \u2014 \u00a7 12.2 anticipates backlog candidates without numbering\n\n> \"Anticipated backlog candidates (NOT yet items, become items only if critique confirms): - Mobile-optimized C3a UI...\"\n\nThese should either be (a) actual backlog items pre-registered now (B-054 through B-058) OR (b) NOT mentioned at all. Mentioning without registering creates the same \"shadow backlog\" problem the actual backlog file exists to prevent.\n\n**My recommendation: REMOVE the anticipated list.** If they emerge as real items in code-build sessions, register them then.\n\n### Finding 11 \u2014 \u00a7 4.5 24h fallback test count says \"8 tests\" but only lists 7 bullet points\n\nReal arithmetic check:\n- happy path enqueue \u2192 fire\n- frozen_clock advance + tick fires it\n- failed POST \u2192 record_error \u2192 re-claim\n- 3 failures \u2192 stuck\n- admin reset clears stuck\n- email hook called with right payload + trace_id\n- (one missing \u2014 likely \"duplicate tick is no-op due to P28\")\n\n**Recommendation: PATCH** in v0.2 \u2014 either reconcile to 7 or add the 8th explicitly.\n\n## PUSHBACK-REJECTED findings (claims I should make but won't)\n\n### Finding NOT-RAISED-1 \u2014 \"Should test against PostgreSQL too?\"\nAlready pushed back per \u00a7 0.3 (a). The real cost isn't CI minutes. It's trust. Once your team's default response to a red build is \"just rerun it,\" you've already lost the battle. Understanding test failure analysis starts with knowing which category you're in. The 6 root cause categories apply across all frameworks, but here's how each one shows up in Playwright specifically. Before jumping to fixes, you need to know which category you're dealing with. Here's how each root cause shows up in real code. This is the big one. Playwright auto-waits on actions like click() and fill(), but it does NOT auto-wait on: ... That's where most timing flakes hide. The fix is almost always replacing snapshot methods with web-first assertions. There's a subtler timing bug worth knowing: missing await on async Playwright calls.\n\nPlaywright's actionability checks do wait for elements to be stable and not covered by other elements. But animation flakes still happen when the app isn't ready even after an element becomes clickable, or when overlays pop up between the readiness check and the actual click. Think of this as a diagnostic signal rather than a root cause on its own -- the Playwright-specific fix is in the overlays section below. Here's a finding that surprises most teams. A study covering 52 projects found that 46.5% of flaky tests are RAFTs (Resource-Affected Flaky Tests). In a RAFT, the test passes or fails based on how much CPU, memory, or I/O the machine has at runtime -- not based on your code. In Playwright, this looks like tests passing on your M1 Pro but failing on a shared 2-core CI runner, inconsistent results across shards, or tests that only fail when running in parallel.\n\nEach CI run is isolated. You can see what broke today. But you can't see whether a test has been flaking for 6 weeks, whether it only fails on Ubuntu runners, or whether it started after a specific commit. Answering those questions requires historical data across multiple runs. Single-run HTML reports can't give you that. Here's what each detection method can and can't tell you: One finding worth knowing: research on co-occurring test failures found that 75% of flaky tests belong to failure clusters. They tend to fail together and share an underlying cause. When 1 test flakes, look at what else failed in the same run. Instead of investigating 100 individual failures, you end up fixing 3 root causes. TestDino's AI-native test intelligence handles this grouping automatically across runs. If you want to query that data directly from your editor, the Playwright MCP server lets you ask \"which tests flaked most this week\" or \"what changed before this test started failing\" without leaving you\n\nCreate a ticket immediately. Not tomorrow. Assign it to a specific person, not \"the team.\" If a quarantined test isn't fixed within 2 sprints, escalate it. Either fix it or delete it. Teams using Playwright test management tools to track quarantine with ownership and due dates resolve flakes far faster than teams tracking them in spreadsheets. Target to aim for: 95-98% pass rate and less than 2% flaky rate. These are operational guidelines, not hard rules. Slack, GitHub, and Atlassian all arrived at the same approach: automate detection, quarantine with an assigned owner and a deadline, and track stability trends across runs rather than single-run reports. The details vary by company. The pattern doesn't vary. Slack built \"Project Cornflake\" to automate detection and suppression, dropping test job failure rate from 57% to under 4%. Atlassian built \"Flakinator\" for the Jira Frontend repo, which auto-quarantines flaky tests, assigns an owner via code ownership, and creates a Jira ticket",

On a team I worked with years ago \u2014 mid-stage B2B SaaS, 12 engineers, 8 services \u2014 the suite had about 1,200 end-to-end tests. Roughly 4% flaked per run. Sounds tolerable.\n\nI shipped a self-healing test framework three years ago. Nobody called it agentic then. The word... Tagged with agents, automation, javascript, testing.\n\nWhat I built: a generator that output Playwright/TypeScript tests from plan Markdown, with locator strategies (data-testid preferred, role-based fallback, text-based last-resort), fixture scaffolding, and soft-assertion patterns baked in. Job: when a test fails, diagnose whether the failure is real (app bug), structural (locator stale after a UI refactor), or environmental (CI flake). Fix the structural ones. Flag the real ones. Quarantine the environmental ones with context. Why it's separate: and this is the one nobody wanted to believe at the time \u2014 healing is not about re-running failed tests until they pass. That's not healing; that's hiding. Real healing is triage plus targeted mutation plus review. What I built: a healer that diffed the current DOM against the last green run, proposed three locator candidates when the old one was stale, scored each against a stability heuristic, and opened a PR with the one-line change for a human to review.\n\nI don't love citing numbers without naming the shop, but my feedback memory is explicit on that. So here's what's defensible: On one project, the three-role pattern let us grow the test suite by ~3\u00d7 over 18 months while the flake rate stayed flat. On another, we cut the test-maintenance time-per-engineer by more than a third in the first quarter after rollout. On a third, the Healer caught a UI-refactor regression pattern (100+ tests stale from a single CSS rename) and produced a single-PR fix overnight. The alternative would have been a 3-week cleanup sprint. These numbers are not magic. They are the mechanical consequence of separating concerns and instrumenting the boundary between them. If you already do this with your services in production, you already know why it works. Explore more \u00b7 Test flakiness often stems from several common issues: Race conditions: Tests proceed before the application is ready, leading to timing failures. Unstable selectors: Using selectors that change between renders or application states. Network unpredictability: Variations in API response times or intermittent failures. State contamination: Tests affecting each other due to shared state. Environmental differences: Tests that only fail in certain environments (CI vs local).\n\nConsider different timeout values for development versus CI environments. With properly configured timeouts, your tests will achieve the right balance between giving operations enough time to complete and failing promptly when something is genuinely wrong. Playwright's retry mechanism serves as a practical defense against test flakiness in production environments. When enabled, this feature automatically re-runs failed tests multiple times, allowing intermittently failing tests to have additional opportunities to pass before being conclusively marked as failures. By default, Playwright runs each test exactly once with no automatic retries. This behavior provides clear results during test development but can create problems in CI/CD pipelines where environmental factors might cause occasional failures in otherwise solid tests. To enable automatic retries, configure the retries parameter in your Playwright configuration: ...\n\nThis article explores comprehensive strategies to minimize test flakiness in Playwright. By implementing these practices, you can build a more reliable test suite that provides genuine confidence in your application's functionality. ... Explore more \u00b7 Test flakiness often stems from several common issues: Race conditions: Tests proceed before the application is ready, leading to timing failures. Unstable selectors: Using selectors that change between renders or application states. Network unpredictability: Variations in API response times or intermittent failures. State contamination: Tests affecting each other due to shared state. Environmental differences: Tests that only fail in certain environments (CI vs local).\n\nCreating reliable Playwright tests requires a multi-faceted approach that addresses the common sources of flakiness. By implementing proper waiting strategies with explicit conditions rather than arbitrary timeouts, you establish a foundation for stability. Choosing stable selectors through data-test attributes insulates your tests from styling changes, while properly mocking network requests ensures consistent behavior regardless of external services. Test isolation ensures each test runs independently with a clean state, while flexible assertions accommodate reasonable UI variations. In CI environments, configuring appropriate retry policies and timeouts acknowledges that these environments often behave differently than development machines. When tests do fail, Playwright's tracing and video recording capabilities provide the visibility needed to diagnose issues quickly.\n\nLearn how to use Playwright with Python for end-to-end web testing. Validate Playwright tests on real browsers and environments using BrowserStack before flaky failures reach CI.\n\nFlaky tests in Playwright are automated tests that pass during one execution and fail the next, even when no changes have been made to the codebase. They create both false positives and false negatives, which waste debugging time and make it harder to catch real issues. Also Read: How to avoid False Positives and False Negatives in Testing? Flaky tests waste time on false failures and slow down releases. Here are some ways they impact CI pipelines. False alarms: Flaky tests cause failures that are not real and make teams spend time investigating issues that do not actually exist. Blocked deployments: When test results feel unreliable teams hesitate to push code which slows down releases and disrupts development flow. Read More: Testing Tactics for Faster Release Cycles \u00b7 Repeated reruns: Tests often need multiple runs to determine if a failure is real or just flaky, which wastes time and resources.\n\nCSS or XPath selectors often break when the UI changes. Playwright locators are more stable because they rely on roles, text, or labels. Use this approach for more reliable element targeting: // Avoid await page.click('#submit-btn'); // Use await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' }).click(); ... Random inputs make tests unreliable because they produce different outcomes each run. Use fixed values or seed your randomness to keep test behavior predictable. ... Tests that depend on shared state can interfere with each other and create flakiness. Isolation tests ensure each test runs independently with a clean setup. ... Validate Playwright tests on real environments using BrowserStack before flaky failures reach CI. ... Some failures come from temporary network or environment issues. Setting retries helps detect flaky tests by rerunning failed ones before marking them as broken.\n\nTrack recurring flaky tests using Playwright\u2019s HTML reports or CI analytics tools. Adding tags like @flaky helps filter these tests and prioritize fixing them based on frequency and impact. Once a flaky test is identified, fix it by targeting the root cause to ensure consistent results: ... Use Playwright\u2019s Trace Viewer to inspect failures in detail. Run the following to review test steps, DOM snapshots, and network activity. ... Replace fragile selectors with Playwright\u2019s role-based or chained locators for stable element targeting. For example: await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' }).click(); ... Remove fixed delays. Instead, wait for specific app conditions like responses or element states: await expect(page.locator('.toast-message')).toHaveText('Saved successfully'); Also Read: Understanding Playwright waitforloadstate ...         "text": "No wonder, the Playwright documentation on waitForTimeout() states: \u201cNever wait for timeout in production. Tests that wait for time are inherently flaky. Use Locator actions and web assertions that wait automatically.\u201d \u00b7 When using a locator, Playwright performs a series of actionability checks on the selected node. Specifically, it automatically waits until all relevant checks are passed and only then performs the requested action on the node. Similarly, when using a web-first assertion, the automation framework automatically waits for a predefined time until the expected condition is met. If these conditions are not met in the expected default time, the test fails with a TimeoutError. Note that you can usually configure default timeouts with a custom timeout option on both locator and assertion functions.\n\nIn this article, you learned what a flaky test is and what implications it has in a CI/CD process. In particular, you saw some Playwright best practices that specifically target the root cause of flakiness. Thanks to them, you can now write robust tests that produces the same results consistently. Although eliminating flaky tests forever is not possible, you should now be able to reduce them as much as possible. Keep your CI pipeline safe from unpredictable failures! A decade of Java development drove me to operations, scaling infrastructure to cope with the thundering herd.\n\nThe simplest and most intuitive way to avoid Playwright flaky tests is to try to prevent them. The idea is to thoroughly inspect all tests locally before committing them. This way, you can ensure that they are robust and work as intended in different scenarios. Since a flaky test may or may not fail by definition, you should run each test more than once and on a machine with similar resources as the production server. Changing the environment on local testing would produce results that are not comparable with those of the CI/CD pipeline. Another rule for identifying such a test is \u201cif it passes on the branch but fails once merged, it may be a flaky test.\u201d In the context of Playwright, a test is labeled as \u201cflaky\u201d when it fails the first time but passes when retried. The impact of Playwright flaky tests is particularly significant in a CI pipeline because of their inconsistent nature, resulting in unpredictable failures for the same commit across various attempts.\n\nNo wonder, the Playwright documentation on waitForTimeout() states: \u201cNever wait for timeout in production. Tests that wait for time are inherently flaky. I spent some time recently on flaky tests, and I wanted to share a few tips on how to easily reproduce those flakiness locally and ensuring you can trust your commit rather than sending multiple commits Fix tests (for real this time). I recently worked on end-to-end tests with Playwright that were loading a web app, but also loading an heavy collaborative editor (read this as something that takes a while to load). On my machine, it was working well all the time (obviously), but on CI, it was failing randomly\u2014but often. I suspected it had to do with timeouts and the fact that the editor was loading a bit slowly, but I wanted to be sure of it, so I started to investigate.\n\nDoing so, I was able to throttle my CPU and I reproduced those flakiness right away. 4 to 6 times worked well for me (M1 Pro), but you can adjust it to your needs. From there, I was able to trace the failures and fix them with confidence. Yes, they worked well on CI too! Since you made it this far, I wanted to share a few other tips I learned while working with Playwright. Retries are a useful feature for retrying flaky tests automatically, but they can be a pain when debugging. You should remember to disable them by setting the retries option to 0 when investigating flakiness, otherwise it could hide some flakiness in those retries. I know, retries are also mentioned in the test results ... An alternative to it, could be to use the explicit --fail-on-flaky-tests option, which will fail the test suite if any test is flagged as flaky. There's a command to repeat each single test N times!\n\nI mentioned earlier that our machines are usually more powerful than CI machines, but what if you want to stress test your machine (other than by throttling)? You can use the --workers option to run multiple tests in parallel, which is useful to stress test your machine and reproduce flakiness. ... Workers represents the number of concurrent workers or percentage of logical CPU cores. E.g., using --workers 1 means there's a single worker, which disables parallelism. By default, Playwright uses 50% of logical CPU cores. ... Reading Playwright logs can be challenging sometimes, especially if you have a lot of them running in parallel. Tagged with a11y, webdev, playwright, ux.\n\nIn this article, we'll show you how we use Playwright combined with Axe (@axe-core/playwright) to automatically catch accessibility issues and integrate them into our CI pipeline. We chose Axe, an open-source library from Deque Systems, as our accessibility testing engine. It's well-regarded, easy to use, and provides a JavaScript API to run tests directly in the browser. The @axe-core/playwright package makes integration seamless. And since we already rely on Playwright for visual regression testing and our end-to-end suite, adding accessibility checks right on top of that felt like the obvious next step. No new tools to learn, just extending a setup we know well with Axe\u2019s engine running inside the same Playwright workflows. First, we created a helper to get a pre-configured Axe instance. Our configuration focuses on WCAG 2.1 Level A and AA criteria.\n\nThe Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG are developed by the W3C to make web content more accessible. Level A: The minimum level of conformance. Level AA: The mid-range level we (and many others) target, as it addresses more advanced barriers. Level AAA: The highest, most stringent level. We also explicitly exclude certain elements that are outside our direct control, such as third-party advertisements positions, to avoid false positives. // From /test/utils/axe.ts import { Page } from '@playwright/test'; import AxeBuilder from '@axe-core/playwright'; const getAxeInstance = (page: Page) => { return new AxeBuilder({ page }) // We decided to target WCAG 2.1 A and AA success criteria .withTags(['wcag2a', 'wcag2aa']) // We exclude elements we don't control, like ads .exclude('[id^=\"google_ads_iframe_\"]') .exclude('#skinadvtop2') .exclude('#subito_skin_id'); }; Next, we implemented another helper function, generateAxeReport, to run the analysis and save the results.\n\nWith these helpers in place, adding an accessibility check to any Playwright test is incredibly simple. // From /test/a11y/example.spec.ts import { test } from '@playwright/test'; import { generateAxeReport } from '../utils/axe'; test('check Login page', async ({ page }) => { await page.goto('/login_form'); await page.waitForLoadState('domcontentloaded'); // Just call our helper! await generateAxeReport('login-page', page, false); }); It generates a JSON report for the login-page, essentially, each test run produces a structured JSON output with all the accessibility findings: Our workflow is triggered every time our staging environment is updated. The action performs the following steps: Runs the accessibility tests against a predefined list of critical pages. Generates the JSON reports. Updates or creates a dedicated GitHub Issue with the results whenever violations are detected.",

This site is open source.\n\nRepository for demonstrating accessibility testing features and workflows\n\nWhat is axe-core? Think of it as as an accessibility-focused test runner that has built-in Rules and Checks for testing accessibility compliance, and can generate Reports in formats suitable for analysis or integration in developer workflows. The workflow has 3 steps: Configuration - use supported options to configure test run \u00b7 Execution - run axe (CLI or integrations) to start test run on site \u00b7 Reporting - view the returned accessibility-results report (JSON) The report provides details on the rules prioritized in test, the number of nodes (matching elements) on which rules were assessed, and the outcomes (passes, violations, incomplete, inapplicable) of those assessments. The report also provides insights into probable causes for violations, and proposes fixes to remediate them. The developer can use the report as guidance to make relevant fixes, then re-run scan to validate that fixes worked and accessibility compliance goals are met.\n\nDeque Systems also maintains axe DevTools HTML a suite of accessibility testing tools built on top of axe-core that make it easy to integrate automated testing into your developer workflow. Key to this is the Shift Left philosophy to move detection and remediation closer to the start of the developer workflow (e.g., design or authoring) instead of at the end (build or post-deployment) - driven by the 4 components below: ... That's not the same thing \u2014 it has none of the DSL drift problem \u00a7 0.3 (g) cites, and it solves a real issue (each test redefining shape assertions = drift surface area).\n\n**My one refinement to their Option A:** put the contract module in `tests/validation/_contract.py` rather than as a top-level `c3a_contract.py`. Let CI rerun the whole job; mark in CI that a retry happened.\n\nPATCH.\n\n### External Item 4 \u2014 Performance budget (P37) fragile\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH (extends my own Finding 5)**\n\nThe external critique goes beyond my \"bump to 120s\" suggestion with a smarter structure: tier separation. `tests/validation/` runs always (PR + CI); `tests/e2e/` is `@pytest.mark.e2e`-tagged and runs only on the slower nightly/full job.\n\nThis actually preserves P37's intent better than just buying budget. CI flake from budget overrun on a PR run is now impossible (validation is fast); the slower e2e gets its own budget headroom.\n\nCombined patch:\n- Validation suite budget: **30 sec hard cap** (was implicitly 60s in my v0.1.1)\n- E2E budget: **60 sec hard cap** (was implicitly 30s)\n- Total: 90s when both run, but they don't always run together\n- `@pytest.mark.e2e` marker; PR runs with `-m \"not e2e\"`; nightly runs everything\n- Soft warning in the test runner if validation > 25s (early signal before hitting cap)\n\nPATCH-EXTENDED.\n\n### External Item 5 \u2014 UI error handling under-specified\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH (extends my own Finding 7)**\n\nThe external critique's structured categorization is better than my \"show 'checking...' immediately\" patch. The race scenario: user clicks \"I confirm\" (POST `/cba-verified`) at the exact moment the scheduler claims that row and POSTs `/cba-fallback-continue`. Both requests carry **different** request_ids (user has theirs, scheduler has the one from scheduler_state), so P21 idempotency on (session_token, request_id) doesn't dedupe across the two paths.\n\nThe protection is supposed to be: once one transition fires (e.g., to CBA_VERIFIED), subsequent transitions return 410. **But** I don't have a test that proves the state-machine check + state mutation are atomic under concurrent access.         "text": "Closing the tab/window ends the session and clears the data in sessionStorage. A Storage object which can be used to access the current origin&#x27;s session storage space. ... The origin is not a valid scheme/host/port tuple. This can happen if the origin uses the file: or data: schemes, for example.\n\nThe read-only sessionStorage property accesses a session Storage object for the current origin. sessionStorage is similar to localStorage; the difference is that while localStorage is partitioned by origin only, sessionStorage is partitioned by both origin and browser tabs (top-level browsing contexts). The data in sessionStorage is only kept for the duration of the page session. Whenever a document is loaded in a particular tab in the browser, a unique page session gets created and assigned to that particular tab. That page session is accessible only in that particular tab. The main document, and all embedded browsing contexts (iframes), are grouped by their origin and each origin has access to its own separate storage area. If the page has an opener, the sessionStorage is initially a copy of the opener's sessionStorage object. However, they are still separate and changes to one do not affect the other.\n\nTo prevent the sessionStorage from being copied, use one of the techniques that remove opener (see Window.opener). A page session lasts as long as the tab or the browser is open, and survives over page reloads and restores. Opening a page in a new tab or window creates a new session with the value of the top-level browsing context, which differs from how session cookies work. Closing the tab/window ends the session and clears the data in sessionStorage. A Storage object which can be used to access the current origin's session storage space. ... The origin is not a valid scheme/host/port tuple. This can happen if the origin uses the file: or data: schemes, for example. The request violates a policy decision. For example, the user has configured the browsers to prevent the page from persisting data. Note that if the user blocks cookies, browsers will probably interpret this as an instruction to prevent the page from persisting data.\n\n// Save data to sessionStorage sessionStorage.setItem(\"key\", \"value\"); // Get saved data from sessionStorage let data = sessionStorage.getItem(\"key\"); // Remove saved data from sessionStorage sessionStorage.removeItem(\"key\"); // Remove all saved data from sessionStorage sessionStorage.clear(); The following example autosaves the contents of a text field, and if the browser is refreshed, restores the text field content so that no writing is lost. ... // Get the text field that we're going to track let field = document.getElementById(\"field\"); // See if we have an autosave value // (this will only happen if the page is accidentally refreshed) if (sessionStorage.getItem(\"autosave\")) { // Restore the contents of the text field field.value = sessionStorage.getItem(\"autosave\"); } // Listen for changes in the text field field.addEventListener(\"change\", () => { // And save the results into the session storage object sessionStorage.setItem(\"autosave\", field.value); }); Note: Please refer to the",

        "text": "In other words, whatever gets stored in the window.localStorage object will not disappear during a reload or reopening of the web page or when users close their browsers. Those data have no expiration time.\n\nWeb Storage is what the JavaScript API browsers provide for storing data locally and securely within a user\u2019s browser. Session and local storage are the two main types of web storage. They are similar to regular properties objects, but they persist (do not disappear) when the webpage reloads. This article aims to show you exactly how the two storage objects work in JavaScript. We will also use a To-Do list exercise to practice using web storage in a web app project. ... Web Storage vs. Cookies: What is the Difference? ... Without further ado, let\u2019s discuss session storage. The session storage object (window.sessionStorage) stores data that persists for only one session of an opened tab. In other words, whatever gets stored in the window.sessionStorage object will not disappear on a reload of the web page. Instead, the computer will delete the stored data only when users close the browser tab or window.\n\nSession storage is a property of the global Window object. So sessionStorage.setItem() is equivalent to window.sessionStorage.setItem(). The local storage object (window.localStorage) stores data that persists even when users close their browser tab (or window). In other words, whatever gets stored in the window.localStorage object will not disappear during a reload or reopening of the web page or when users close their browsers. Those data have no expiration time. Browsers never clear them automatically. The computer will delete the window.localStorage object\u2019s content in the following instances only: When the content gets cleared through JavaScript \u00b7 When the browser\u2019s cache gets cleared \u00b7 Note the following: The window.localStorage object\u2019s storage limit is larger than the window.sessionStorage. The data stored inside the local storage is per-origin.\n\nSession storage is a property of the global Window object. So sessionStorage.setItem() is equivalent to window.sessionStorage.setItem(). Those data have no expiration time. Browsers never clear them automatically. The computer will delete the window.localStorage object\u2019s content in the following instances only: ... The window.localStorage object\u2019s storage limit is larger than the window.sessionStorage. The data stored inside the local storage is per-origin. In other words, http://freecodecamp.com\u2019s localStorage object is different from https://freecodecamp.com\u2019s localStorage object because the two origins use different schemes (http and https).\n\n// Store carColor: \"Pink\" inside the browser's local storage object: localStorage.setItem(\"carColor\", \"Pink\"); // Store pcColor: \"Yellow\" inside the local storage object: localStorage.setItem(\"pcColor\", \"Yellow\"); // Store laptopColor: \"White\" inside the local storage object: localStorage.setItem(\"laptopColor\", \"White\"); // Clear all items from the local storage: localStorage.clear(); // Confirm whether the local storage still contains any item: console.log(localStorage); // The invocation above will return: {length: 0} ... I would therefore suggest that a very large number of web developers, working on this understanding, are misleading their users within their privacy/GDPR policies. The persistency of data that a website/webapp collects and saves on the user's machine (generally known as a cookie or cookie-like technology) is clearly an important thing for a user to consider when deciding whether to accept a type of cookie and therefore an important thing for website owners to get right when they write their privacy/GDPR webpages. SECURITY Disclosure: I am not a data security expert. Nonetheless, I believe - and I think it is common sense - that all cookies (including session cookies) increase what is known as the 'attack surface' of the websit\n\nTherefore, the fact that, in the case of Firefox, website users are being mislead (by erroneous privacy/GDPR policies - see above) regarding the persistency of session cookies is hindering these users' ability to make informed decisions about the safety of the cookies they are asked to accept. CONCLUSION I believe that if this is not resolved by Firefox, this is something that should be escalated to law makers and other authorities as the impact on data protection and security is severe and widespread. ... GET is idempotent. POST is not. It\u2019s up to you to make sure that your web app logic can handle scenarios like Diane\u2019s, where the POST comes in more than once. The client\u2019s GET request is supposed to be idempotent, even if what YOU do with the data causes side-effects. Always keep in mind the difference between the HTTP GET method and your servlet\u2019s doGet() method.         "text": "The important distinction here is that the user did not request the side-effects, so therefore cannot be held accountable for them. Idempotency means that multiple identical requests will have the same outcome. So it does not matter if a request is sent once or multiple times.\n\nIdempotency and safety are properties of HTTP methods. The HTTP RFC defines these properties and tells us which HTTP methods are safe and idempotent. Server application should make sure to implement the safe and idempotent semantic correctly as clients might expect it. HTTP methods are considered safe if they do not alter the server state. So safe methods can only be used for read-only operations. The HTTP RFC defines the following methods to be safe: GET, HEAD, OPTIONS and TRACE. In practice it is often not possible to implement safe methods in a way they do not alter any server state. \n\nFor example, a GET request might create log or audit messages, update statistic values or trigger a cache refresh on the server. ... Naturally, it is not possible to ensure that the server does not generate side-effects as a result of performing a GET request; in fact, some dynamic resources consider that a feature.\n\nThe important distinction here is that the user did not request the side-effects, so therefore cannot be held accountable for them. Idempotency means that multiple identical requests will have the same outcome. So it does not matter if a request is sent once or multiple times. The following HTTP methods are idempotent: GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, TRACE, PUT and DELETE. All safe HTTP methods are idempotent but PUT and DELETE are idempotent but not safe. Note that idempotency does not mean that the server has to respond in the same way on each request. For example, assume we want to delete a project by an ID using a DELETE request: ... \n\nAs response we might get an HTTP 200 status code indicating that the project has been deleted successfully. If we send this DELETE request again, we might get an HTTP 404",
