        "text": "No, POST is not inherently idempotent. Making the same POST request multiple times can result in different outcomes or create multiple instances of submitted data. ... Terence Bennett, CEO of DreamFactory, has a wealth of experience in government ...\n\nHTTP methods, or verbs, are the actions used to interact with resources in web-based systems, defining the type of operation a client wants to perform. Idempotency is an important property of some HTTP methods, ensuring that executing the same request multiple times produces the same result as if it was performed only once. GET, HEAD, PUT, DELETE, OPTIONS, and TRACE are idempotent methods, meaning they are safe to be retried or executed multiple times without causing unintended side effects. In contrast, POST and PATCH are generally considered non-idempotent, as their outcomes may vary with each request. \n\nUnderstanding and leveraging the idempotent nature of specific HTTP methods helps developers create more consistent, reliable, and predictable web applications and APIs. Here are the HTTP methods and whether each one is idempotent or not. POST is an HTTP (Hypertext Transfer Protocol) method used to submit data to a specified resource, typically for creating or updating content on a ser\n\nIt is often employed when submitting form data, uploading files, or sending JSON payloads to an API. Unlike other HTTP methods like GET, PUT, or DELETE, POST is not inherently idempotent. This means that sending the same POST request multiple times can result in different outcomes or create multiple instances of the submitted data. Consequently, developers need to be cautious when handling POST requests, implementing mechanisms such as idempotency keys or other safeguards to prevent unintended side effects in cases where duplicate requests are submitted or retried. \n\nThe GET method is one of the fundamental HTTP methods used to request data from a specific resource on the web. When a client sends a GET request to a server, it is askin",

Note: While servers are very much encouraged to adhere to the ...\n\nThe HTTP specification only defines idempotency in terms of the intended effect of the client on the server. For example, a POST request intends to send data to the server, whereas a DELETE request intends to delete a resource on the server. In practice, it falls to the server to make sure the routes it exposes adhere to these semantics. Note: While servers are very much encouraged to adhere to the semantics laid out by the HTTP specification, the spec does not mandate it. Nothing is preventing a server in the wild from exposing a non-idempotent endpoint under an idempotent HTTP method, although clients may be surprised. ... \n\nA request with an idempotent method does not necessarily mean that the request has no side effects on the server, only that the client intended none: For example, the server may log the time each request is received.         "text": "The important distinction here is that the user did not request the side-effects, so therefore cannot be held accountable for them. Methods can also have the property of &quot;idempotence&quot; in that (aside from error or expiration issues) the side-effects of N &gt; 0 identical requests is the same as ...\n\nThe important distinction here is that the user did not request the side-effects, so therefore cannot be held accountable for them. Methods can also have the property of \"idempotence\" in that (aside from error or expiration issues) the side-effects of N > 0 identical requests is the same as for a single request. The methods GET, HEAD, PUT and DELETE share this property. Also, the methods OPTIONS and TRACE SHOULD NOT have side effects, and so are inherently idempotent. However, it is possible that a sequence of several requests is non- idempotent, even if all of the methods executed in that sequence are idempotent. \n\nThese methods ought to be considered \"safe\". This allows user agents to represent other methods, such as POST, PUT and DELETE, in a special way, so that the user is made aware of the fact that a possibly unsafe action is being requested. Naturally, it is not possible to ensure that the server does not generate side-effects as a result of performing a GET \n\nThe semantics of the GET method change to a \"conditional GET\" if the request message includes an If-Modified-Since, If-Unmodified-Since, If-Match, If-None-Match, or If-Range header field. A conditional GET method requests that the entity be transferred only under the circumstances described by the conditional header field(s). The conditional GET method is intended to reduce unnecessary network usage by allowing cached entities to be refreshed without requiring multiple requests or transferring data already held by the client.",

Same posture as /api/brief/resume.\n\n       Threat model:\n       - An attacker who has the session_token has equivalent\n         access via the existing /resolve, /abort,\n         /cba-verified endpoints (also unauthed). In particular, assume that V users independently select strings stochastically from a finite, but potentially large, list. An inquisitor who does not know which strings have been selected wishes to identify U of them. \n\nThe inquisitor knows the selection probabilities of each user and is equipped with a method that enables the testing of each (user, string) pair, one at a time, for whether that string had been selected by that user.\n\n\n\nHere we establish that, unless U = V, there is no general strategy that minimizes the distribution of the number of guesses, but in the asymptote as the strings become long we prove the following: by construction, there is an asymptotically optimal class of strategies; the number of guesses required in an asymptotically optimal strategy satisfies a Large Deviation Principle with a rate function, which is not necessarily convex, that can be determined from the rate functions of optimally guessing individual users' strings; if all users' selection statistics are identical, the exponential growth rate of the average guesswork as the stringlength increases is determined by the specific R\u00e9nyi entropy of the string-source with parameter (V-U+1)/(V-U+2), generalizing the known V=U=1 case; and that the Shannon entropy of the source is a lower bound on the average guesswork growth rate for all U and V, thus providin",

Read Consistency: Readers in WAL mode access the unchanged database file, ensuring they ...\n\nBy default, SQLite uses a rollback journal mode to ensure atomicity, consistency, isolation, and durability (ACID) compliance. However, SQLite offers an alternative journaling mode called Write-Ahead Logging (WAL), which dramatically improves write performance and allows concurrent read and write operations. In this post, we\u2019ll dive deep into SQLite\u2019s WAL mode, explaining how it works, when to use it, and its pros and cons. We\u2019ll also explore benchmarks to see how it performs in different scenarios. The Write-Ahead Logging (WAL) mode in SQLite is an advanced journaling mechanism designed to improve performance and concurrency. \n\nIn the default rollback journal mode, every write transaction locks the entire database, preventing concurrent reads and writes. WAL mode, on the other hand, writes changes to a separate WAL file, allowing reads and writes to occur simultaneously.\n\nPeriodic Merging: During a checkpoint, SQLite applies the changes from the WAL file to the main database. Checkpointing can happen automatically or manually. Optimization: You can tune when checkpointing occurs (e.g., by adjusting the size of the WAL file) to balance performance and durability. ... Concurrent Reads/Writes: Unlike rollback journal mode, where writes block reads, WAL mode allows multiple readers and one writer to work concurrently. Read Consistency: Readers in WAL mode access the unchanged database file, ensuring they get a consistent view of the data. WAL mode offers significant benefits in various scenarios, but it\u2019s not suitable for every use case. Here are some ideal situations where WAL mode excels: ... \n\nHowever, the WAL file is truncated periodically, so this is usually a manageable issue. ... If your application is single-t",

        "title": "SQLite concurrent writes and \"database is locked\" errors",
        "url": "https://tenthousandmeters.com/blog/sqlite-concurrent-writes-and-database-is-locked-errors/",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",
          "site_domain": "tenthousandmeters.com",

        "is_missing": false,
        "text": "What I'd like to do in this post is better understand how concurrent writes impact SQLite performance, how many are ok, and how we can improve it. We'll go deep, so let me quickly remind you of the most important facts about SQLite: SQLite is an embedded SQL database engine.\n\nTo see why certain transactions block each other while others don't, let me describe how reads and writes work in WAL mode. SQLite provides transaction isolation meaning that a transaction in progress doesn't see changes made by concurrent transactions. A read transaction \"remembers\" the database state at the point it started. In WAL mode, this is achieved by associating every read transaction with a point in the WAL called the end mark. Its meaning is that the transaction simply ignores new WAL changes appended after the end mark. The transaction reconstructs the database state from the database file and the WAL changes up to the end mark. \n\nThus, there can be many readers, each having its own end mark and its own view of the database state. Writers do not interfere with readers because they simply append changes to the WAL. One thing left: at some point the WAL changes must be transferred to the database file since read performance deteriorates as the WAL grows.\n\nIt cannot transfer WAL changes that go after the end mark of any active transaction. If it did, it would update the database file with changes that the transaction should not see, violating isolation guarantees. So checkpointing runs only up to the first end mark. Another thing is that checkpointing cannot shrink the WAL while there are active readers. So it can run concurrently with readers, but it will never complete if there is always at least one active reader, and the WAL file will grow indefinitely. This problem is known as checkpoint starvation. It has multiple solutions including manual checkpointing. \n\nYou can learn more about it and other WAL mode specifics in the docs. In most cases, a write transaction only takes millisec",
        "is_citable": true,
        "prompt_context_metadata": {
          "url": "https://tenthousandmeters.com/blog/sqlite-concurrent-writes-and-database-is-locked-errors/",

        "text": "In the diagram below, we have the logical view of a b-tree inside SQLite and an associated WAL file with 3 transactions. Before the WAL file exists, we have three pages in our database, represented in black. The first transaction (in green) updates pages 1 and 2. A snapshot of the database after this transaction can be constructed by using WAL entries for pages 1 and 2 and the original page from the database for page 3. Our second transaction (in red) only updates page 2. If we want a snapshot after this transaction, we\u2019ll use page 1 from the first transaction, page 2 from the second transaction, and page 3 from the database file. The last transaction (in orange) updates pages 2 &amp; 3 so the entire b-tree is now read from the WAL. The beauty of this approach is that we are no longer overwriting our pages so every transaction can reconstruct its original state from when it started.\n\n\n\nYou can add these photos to a binder and now the inventory folks can see a point-in-time snapshot of the ingredients without interfering with the sandwich maker. This is how the WAL works, in concept. Let\u2019s create a sandwiches.db that will store our current count of ingredients: ... Internally, this command performs a rollback journal transaction to update the database header so it\u2019s safe to use like any other transaction. The database header has a read & write version at bytes 18 & 19, respectively, that are used to determine the journal mode. They\u2019re set to 0x01 for rollback journal and 0x02 for write-ahead log. They\u2019re typically both set to the same value. Now that we are using the WAL, we can add our initial inventory counts: ...         "text": "Here we list the different types of operations and whether or not they can potentially cause conflicts: ... Such a query will never cause another transaction to conflict and itself will always have a consistent view of the database throughout its lifetime thus it cannot generate conflicts. ... Like the singular read operation above, this trans\n\nVersatile as it is, SQLite still suffers from one major drawback. Which is write concurrency. SQLite, using the Write-Ahead-Log (WAL) journaling mode, supports an unlimited number of readers and a single writer any given moment. Thus all write operations are effectively serialized. And while SQLite is very fast at performing writes, it gets stuck in the fsync operation required to ensure the durability of the data after each transaction (the D in ACID). \n\nEngines with a server component that can coordinate concurrent writes are able to group writes together and spread the cost of fsync operations on multiple transactions while still maintaining consistency. In this article we will explore concurrency modes available for SQLite and touch on a new one I am working on at the moment. Built -for now- specifically for Ruby applications but should be portable to other languages as well.\n\nLocks are now taken only at commit phase, allowing for much faster writes than WAL mode \u00b7 Very coarse grained, causing many transactions to conflict when in fact they don\u2019t \u00b7 Unfriendly to auto increment or even rowid based primary keys (will conflict often) Unfriendly to indexes on data the increase/decrease with time (e.g. dates and timestamps) Requires handling of SQLITE_SNAPSHOT errors in the application code \u00b7 Requires compiling a specific SQLite branch and not the main/official one \u00b7 HCTree is a new high concurrency btree for SQLite.",

When a read transaction starts, that reader continues to see an unchanging 'snapshot' of the database file as it existed at the moment in time when the read transaction started. Any write transactions that commit while the read transaction is active are still invisible to the read transaction.\"\n\nA read of gate_states is atomic \u2014 readers either see the pre-write state or the post-write state, never partial. The \"mid-update read\" scenario the critic describes is impossible in WAL mode at row granularity.\n\n**However:** the critic's \"Option B\" (\"Store state as single JSON blob (already mostly true) \u2014 ensure writes are atomic (replace, not mutate)\") is what we already do. `gate_states.state_json` is a single column, replaced atomically. This is the contract\n       that future S7b changes must respect; if backend status\n       semantics change (e.g., a new status code introduced),\n       this table updates and UI behavior follows.\n\n       | HTTP status         | UI category    | UX behavior   |\n       |---------------------|----------------|---------------|\n       | 200                 | (success)      | onSuccess()   |\n       | 400, 422            | USER_FIXABLE   | error + hint  |\n       | 401, 403            | USER_FIXABLE   | error msg     |\n       | 404 unknown_token   | TERMINAL       | session-not-found redirect |\n       | 410 gone (terminal) | TERMINAL       | done-page redirect |\n       | 429 rate-limited    | RETRYABLE      | \"wait\" UI + manual retry |\n       | 500-502, 504        | RETRYABLE      | \"system error\" + manual retry |\n       | 503 storage_busy    | RETRYABLE      | auto-retry once with Retry-After |\n       | network/CORS error  | RETRYABLE      | \"no connection\" + manual retry |\n\n       This mapping is the API\u2194UI contract surface. I'm playing the adversarial critic.\n\n---\n\n# Round 3 Critique \u2014 Focused Spot-Check on Round-2 Changes\n\nScope per protocol: review the v0.2 \u2192 v0.3 changes only, not re-walk earlier sections. The 9 changes are P43, /check-init wrapper, status threat model, raw cross-check tests, looped concurrency, /status snapshot isolation note, status-code mapping table, a11y rename + checklist, BUILDEMUP_PUBLIC_URL validation, test budget hygiene rule, flow controller.\n\n## Findings\n\n### Finding R3.1 \u2014 P43 trace_id implementation has a hidden contract drift risk\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT**\n\nP43 changes `_resolve_external_trace_id` to return a tuple `(server_trace_id, client_trace_id)` (or similar). But existing S7b call sites in c3a_endpoint.py wrappers expect a single trace_id return \u2014 they capture it into a local variable used both for response body AND metric emission.\n\nThe spec doesn't specify which trace_id appears in:\n- Response body `trace_id` field (currently single field)\n- Metric `c3a.endpoint` line `trace_id` field\n\nIf the spec doesn't pin this down, code-build will guess \u2014 and round 4 will catch the inconsistency.\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH-LITE.** Add explicit per-surface mapping:\n- Response body `trace_id` field = client_trace_id (what user sees)\n- Metric line `trace_id` field = server_trace_id (what observability uses)\n- Both logged in structured request log lines\n\n### Finding R3.2 \u2014 /check-init wrapper doesn't specify request_id handling\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT**\n\n\u00a7 5.2 says case.html does `GET /api/extreme-case/check-init?brief=<token>&request_id=<new-uuid>`. But:\n- Browser refresh re-issues the same GET URL with the SAME request_id \u2192 P21 idempotency returns cached response (correct).\n- Browser back/forward \u2192 same URL, same request_id \u2192 cached response (correct).\n- BUT: if case.html mints a new uuid client-side on each load (no persistence), refresh creates a NEW request_id \u2192 NOT cached \u2192 new session_token created \u2192 orphan session in DB.\n\nThe spec needs to say: case.html generates request_id deterministically from the brief_token (or stores it in URL/sessionStorage so refresh preserves it).\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH.** Specify request_id derivation: `request_id = sha256(brief_token + page_load_marker)[:32]` OR store in URL on first request and re-use on refresh. Pick one explicitly.\n\n### Finding R3.3 \u2014 Looped concurrency tests will violate determinism (P36)\n\n**Severity: IMPORTANT**\n\nThe new pattern is `@pytest.mark.parametrize(\"iteration\", range(50))` with `random.uniform(0, 0.01)` delays. Random delays = non-deterministic timing = potential for one iteration in 50 to fail by chance even when the code is correct.\n\nTension with P36 (<0.5% flake rate target): 50 iterations with random timing across 3 race tests = 150 race executions per CI run. Even at a true 0.1% race-failure rate, that's roughly 1 in 7 runs failing.\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH.** Two options:\n- (a) Seed `random` with a known value at start of each iteration (`random.seed(iteration)`). Deterministic but covers timing variation across iterations. **Recommended.**\n- (b) Reduce loop count to 10 with seeded delays.\n\nThe original critique (round-2 X5) was right that fixed-timing tests miss race conditions, but unconstrained randomness violates P36. Seeded randomness is the principled fix.\n\n### Finding R3.4 \u2014 flow controller signature doesn't match error-categorization model\n\n**Severity: NICE-TO-HAVE**\n\n\u00a7 5.6 X11 defines:\n```javascript\nasync function handlePageFlow({ apiCall, onSuccess, errorContainer }) {\n  ...\n  catch (err) {\n    displayError(err, traceId(), errorContainer);\n  }\n}\n```\n\nBut TERMINAL errors (404/410) per \u00a7 5.6 should auto-redirect, not display. The simple `displayError` call doesn't handle that.\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH-LITE.** Update controller to:\n```javascript\ncatch (err) {\n  if (err.type === 'TERMINAL') {\n    handleTerminalRedirect(err);  // redirects per status\n  } else {\n    displayError(err, traceId(), errorContainer);\n  }\n}\n```\n\n### Finding R3.5 \u2014 Test budget hygiene rule lacks enforcement mechanism\n\n**Severity: NICE-TO-HAVE**\n\n\u00a7 4.0.1 says \"Each new test added in any future critique round MUST justify what invariant it protects.\" Aspirational rule, no enforcement. Without a CI check (e.g., a linter that fails if a test class lacks docstring), this rule will erode.\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 DEFERRED.** Add a backlog candidate B-NEW for \"automate test-justification linting.\" Don't try to spec the linter now (Pattern E counter \u2014 scope already growing).\n\n### Finding R3.6 \u2014 \u00a7 5a.5 threat model \"do not forward\" warning is half a fix\n\n**Severity: NICE-TO-HAVE**\n\nThe PATCH adds a \"do not forward this email\" warning text. Verified industry pattern, but: users routinely forward emails despite warnings. The warning is UX security theater. Real protection is short-lived view tokens (B-042) \u2014 already deferred.\n\n**Verdict: PARTIAL \u2014 keep warning (cheap, doesn't hurt) but DON'T claim it's threat mitigation.** Update language: \"minor UX hint, not security.\"\n\n### Finding R3.7 \u2014 No round 3 finding on referrer meta tag \u2014 but it has a known issue\n\n**Severity: NICE-TO-HAVE / web-verifiable**\n\nI should have flagged this myself: `no-referrer-when-downgrade` is the BROWSER DEFAULT in modern browsers (Firefox 87+, Chrome 85+). Adding the meta tag explicitly does nothing the browser doesn't already do.\n\nStronger choice: `<meta name=\"referrer\" content=\"strict-origin-when-cross-origin\">` or `<meta name=\"referrer\" content=\"no-referrer\">`.\n\n**Verdict: VALID \u2014 PATCH-LITE.** Update \u00a7 5a.5 to specify `no-referrer` (paranoid; no Referer ever sent for C3a pages) \u2014 token never leaks via Referer at all.\n\n## Summary\n\n| # | Finding | Severity | Verdict |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| R3.1 | P43 trace_id surface mapping unspecified | IMPORTANT | PATCH-LITE |\n| R3.2 | /check-init request_id handling unspecified | IMPORTANT | PATCH |\n| R3.3 | Looped concurrency violates P36 determinism | IMPORTANT | PATCH (seed random) |\n| R3.4 | Flow controller doesn't handle TERMINAL | NICE | PATCH-LITE |\n| R3.5 | Test hygiene rule lacks enforcement | NICE | DEFERRED \u2192 B-NEW |\n| R3.6 | \"Do not forward\" warning oversold | NICE | PARTIAL (relabel) |\n| R3.7 | Referrer policy uses browser default | NICE | PATCH-LITE (use no-referrer) |\n\n**Score: 7 findings. 3 IMPORTANT, 4 NICE.** No CRITICAL.\n\nPer Option B protocol \u2014 \"LOCK as v1.0 if round 3 is clean (\u22643 minor findings).\" We have 3 IMPORTANT findings. **Not clean enough to LOCK as-is.**\n\nBut: all 3 IMPORTANT findings are TIGHT scope \u2014 surgical fixes to v0.3 sections, not architectural rethinks.
